# Evidence Ledger

**Standalone appendix to** *[TITLE]* (THEIA Services, September 6, 2026; revised [DATE])

This appendix records the evidentiary basis for every substantive claim in the note, the direction changes made during the project, the scope of each negative claim, and the limitations that survive publication. It is written to be checkable: each row names a source, a citation, and an independent corroboration or its absence.

---

## Coverage caveat governing this ledger

**Updated October 13, 2026. Verification of the NEEDS VERIFICATION rows; details in `verification_v4_log.md`.** Checked so far: [CM] Federal Register pinpoints, [DL4] comment IDs, [DL3] re-check list; 90 of the 91 flagged rows. Each claim was compared with the primary source the row names, and every quotation and matched phrase was found by script in the downloaded text (Federal Register and statute: govinfo.gov PDFs and HTML, with OCR for tables printed as images; comments: regulations.gov API and attachments). Outcome: 34 rows match and lose the flag (25 upgraded, for example single-source to verified); 8 differ and are marked **corrected**, with the source's wording (24 words or fewer), the pinpoint and what v17 must change; 48 keep the flag with 'blocked: <host>' or 'not found'. 0 rows with two tags were resolved for one tag only and keep the other tag's flag (counted above by the result for the resolved tag). No row text other than the Cross-check and Status columns was changed. The appendix title line now names THEIA Services instead of the [FIRM] placeholder.

**Updated October 11, 2026. Section A has been synced to the October 2026 draft of the note (v17), which is now its source of truth for Section A.** This replaces the September 27 statement below that the ledger is synced to v9. The note was revised after the v9 sync through the public-comment review (verification summary, decision log and change map). Because the ledger is offered to the note's readers on request, any Section A row that contradicts the draft is treated as a defect. This update:

- **Added.** 73 rows cover claims v17 makes that had no row: RM 18, CE 25, SB 8, TP 12, SE 3, AC 1, NC 6. They include the 91 FR 30410 financing shares from CMS's analysis of 402 approved SDPs (CE-80), the $315.9B combined state figure and the states' net of -$147.5B, 91 FR 30462, the CMS-CBO reconciliation and CBO's two measures (SB-26 to SB-30), the year-basis caveat (CE-57 to CE-59), and the public-comment material with comment IDs (TP-29 to TP-40, SB-31 to SB-33, NC-17).
- **Updated.** 121 existing rows were reworded or restated to match v17; each records "UPDATED October 11, 2026" in the cross-check column and, where the claim changed, the previous wording. A further 3 rows (SB-02, SB-03, TP-14) are unchanged except for a NEEDS VERIFICATION flag. Two of the updates are corrections of the v3 ledger itself: **SE-16** recorded KFF's base-rate finding backwards (states with most SDP spending above the caps already have base rates at or near the caps; low base rates leave more SDP spending under them), and **CE-32** now records CMS's central estimate as a 65 percent tax-financed share, up to 80 percent of the cut on SDPs and a 30 percent state offset, with the low-savings (75/90/40) and high-savings (55/70/20) sets. Other corrections of the v3 ledger found on the way: SB-09 said $332.1B no longer appears in the note (App. F states it); RM-04 and RM-21 carried the 6,344 count as a posted count (App. I treats it as the Federal Register's own count); SE-18 used "phase-down" for the provider tax threshold.
- **Superseded.** 27 more rows are marked SUPERSEDED (RM-22, CE-22, CE-35, CE-44, CE-46, CE-47, CE-53, SB-19, SB-21, SB-23, SB-25, TP-02, TP-03, TP-09, TP-22, TP-25, TP-27, TP-28, SE-15, SE-19, AC-06, AC-13, NC-10, NC-13, NC-14, FG-04, FG-09), making 46 with the 19 already marked. Each keeps its original text and records the reason, what removed it where the supplied materials say, and its replacement row. Nothing is deleted.
- **Needs verification.** 91 rows carry "NEEDS VERIFICATION" at the end of the Status column, with a tag: [DL3] the item is on the decision log's re-check list or the change map's citation-to-add or re-check lists (60 rows); [DL4] the row cites a public-comment ID that is on the change map's "citation to add after verification" list (16 rows); [CM] the Federal Register pinpoint is one the change map marks "pinpoint from new-text check; re-check in the ledger delta" (17 rows). A row can carry more than one tag. The flag does not change the row's base status.
- **Tags.** The ledger has no tag column. For added and updated rows the note's evidence tag ([E] stated in a cited public document; [I] THEIA Services' arithmetic or reading; [C] depends on facts not yet public) is appended to the Type column. Rows this update did not touch carry no tag.
- **Numbering.** v17 has no footnotes and renumbers the sections: the former sec. 3a-3d (the $56.6B, $681B, sector and ASPE subsections) are now sec. 5; the former sec. 4 (state exposure) is sec. 6; the former sec. 5 (access) is sec. 8; the former sec. 7 (what to watch) is sec. 9; the former footnotes 1-3 are in App. E and footnotes 4-5 are gone (the five-axis list is not in v17; the group definitions are in sec. 6 and App. H). v17 also says "central", "low-savings" and "high-savings" estimates where the ledger says primary, low and high. Rows this update touched use v17's numbering and terms. Pointers inside rows it did not touch, and Sections B to D, still use the v9 numbering and are not remapped.
- **Not re-synced.** Sections B to D are historic records and keep their original text. The figure artwork was not supplied, so Cluster 8 rows are checked against v17's captions only. Rows citing comment IDs rest on the comment analyst's output, which the verification files do not cover, and are single-source or flagged. Federal Register pinpoints read only in the new-text check are single-source until re-checked. Rows whose subject v17 only outlines (App. C-F) are retained with a note rather than superseded.


**Updated September 27, 2026. This ledger has been synced to the published note (v9), which is its source of truth.** The note was revised four times after the September 22 sync, including a restructure of section 3 and two adversarial review passes (D-14, D-15), which overturned several claims this ledger had recorded as verified. Because the ledger is offered to the note's readers on request, any row that contradicts the published note is treated as a defect. This update:

- **Cross-references.** Every section, item and footnote reference now follows the note's current numbering. Section 3: 3a is the $56.6 billion figure, 3b the $681 billion figure, 3c the sector's distinctions, 3d ASPE. Section 7: items 1 to 11 (former items 4a, 4b and 4c are now 5, 6 and 7; former item 6 is now 9; former item 8 is now 11). Footnotes: the five-axis footnote is now footnote 4, and the group-definitions footnote is now footnote 5. Rows were matched to claims by content, not by old number. Historical entries in Sections B to D keep their original references, with the current number added in parentheses.
- **Superseded, not deleted.** Eighteen more rows are marked SUPERSEDED, making nineteen with AC-05. Each keeps its original text and records the reason, the revision or review that removed or reversed the claim, and its replacement row.
- **Added.** Sixty-one rows cover claims the note now makes and the ledger did not record, including the stacked combined figure and its bounds, CMS's caveat at 91 FR 30453, the pre-statute baseline, the Table 12 caption, and one row for each of the note's nine figures (new Cluster 8).
- **Verifications.** Three external verifications are recorded at D-16.
- **Title.** The note's published title line is a placeholder, "[TITLE]", with "revised [DATE]" in its date line. The ledger's title line reproduces the note as published. It will need updating when the note's title is filled in.

New worksheet identifiers cited by the added rows (for example R0515, R0581–R0636, R0780–R0789, R0956–R0965, R0984–R0989, R1009, R1019) were read directly from the worksheet for this update. Federal Register quotations that the worksheet does not transcribe (CE-40, and the scenario-parameter wording at CE-48) are recorded as single-source or with a caveat.

**Updated September 9, 2026.** This ledger was first built on September 7, 2026 against an earlier revision of the note, and against an incomplete set of inputs. Two things have changed.

**1. The reconciliation worksheet (C1) is now available, and the row identifiers have been verified.** The September 7 ledger carried every identifier of the form (R0702) forward from the note's own text without checking it. All 47 distinct R-numbers cited in the note and in this ledger have now been resolved against the worksheet's Reconciliation sheet, and every one of them matches on rule, table, Federal Register page, value and series. Two negative claims that rested on the worksheet's coverage — TP-07 and NC-11, with NC-08 — have been re-checked directly against it and upgraded. Federal Register pages and table numbers are still reproduced as the note states them; none has been re-read against the Federal Register or govinfo.

**Two passages the note cites are not in the worksheet, and are cited to the Federal Register page directly instead:** CMS's stated range of "between 55 and 75 percent" (CE-31) and five of the six scenario parameters at CE-32. The worksheet transcribes quantitative estimates; these are stated assumptions, and only the 65 percent primary parameter (R1012) was transcribed. This is a scope boundary of the worksheet, not an error in either document.

**2. The note has been revised twice since the September 7 ledger, and the ledger has been brought current.** The unledgered material was CMS's stated tax-financing range and its three scenario parameter sets, the derived low- and high-scenario overlaps, the rewritten section 5 with its three access citations, the rewritten rural paragraph, the corrected count of flagged states, and the tightened wording of section 6's third gap. Fifteen rows have been added, eight updated and one marked superseded; the changes are summarised at D-11.

**Cross-checks against state-level data** were performed against the state tier file (C4) rather than the indicator file (C2), which remains unavailable. The tier file carries expansion status, the hospital tax indicator, the share above limits and the data quality flags for all 51 jurisdictions, and every state count in the note reproduces exactly from it, including the corrected count of twelve at SE-19. The dollar column does not, because the tier file carries shares rather than dollars.

Arithmetic identities were recomputed independently for this ledger and are reported in the cross-check column as "Internal." Every identity the note asserts closes.

**Status vocabulary.** *Verified* — corroborated by an independent source or by an arithmetic identity that closes. *Verified with caveat* — corroborated, with a qualification that changes how the claim should be read. *Single-source* — one source, no corroboration located. *Unresolved* — the claim does not close, or no supporting source was located. *Superseded* — the note no longer makes the claim; the row is retained with the revision that removed it. *Corrected* (added October 13, 2026) — the source the row names differs from the claim; the Status records the source's wording (24 words or fewer), the pinpoint and what v17 must change, and the Cross-check gives the base status it replaces. *Needs verification* — a flag appended to the Status column ([DL3], [DL4] or [CM], explained in the October 11, 2026 update above); the row's base status stands until the item is re-checked. After the October 13, 2026 check, a flag that remains ends with 'blocked: <host>' (the source's host refused the connection) or 'not found'.

At 30414 CMS lists CAHs, certain cancer hospitals and freestanding children's hospitals as paid by Medicare on a cost basis and proposes that the most recent Medicare cost report serve as the basis for the total published Medicare payment rate and so the SDP payment limit; it limits the state plan approved rate to providers not paid by Medicare at all, and says the payment limit for cost-paid providers would default to the State plan approved rate only under an alternative it did not propose. 91 FR 30450 (RIA) gives the general rule: the limit is the State plan approved rate where no total published Medicare payment rate exists; the statute says the same at § 71116(a), 139 Stat. 303 (re-check-list part, matched by script). v17's claim that the three hospital types' cap defaults to the state plan rate, and AC-16's [I] reading built on it, do not match the proposal. | corrected — source says CMS proposes, for hospitals Medicare pays on cost: “we propose to use the most recent and complete Medicare cost report … for the purpose of establishing the prospective SDP payment limit” (91 FR 30414). The state plan rate default for these hospitals is an alternative CMS considered and did not propose (91 FR 30414); 91 FR 30450 states the general default where no Medicare rate exists. v17 must rewrite sec. 8, rural ¶2 (see verification_v4_log.md). Base status was single-source. Re-check-list part (re-checked October 13, 2026): the statute's default to the state plan rate, where no total published Medicare rate exists, is P.L. 119-21 § 71116(a), 139 Stat. 303. |

It also reads the 75 percent upper end as 38 + 37 and says CMS does not explain the 55 percent lower end or the 65 percent point estimate. This row's statements that the 65 percent is a share of 'SDP spending', and that CMS publishes no weighting for 'the range endpoints', conflict with both. Sec. 71116 has no revenue effect, so its outlay and deficit figures are both $149.4B. Outlays: 191.1 + 149.4 = 340.5, which rounds to POLITICO's $340B. Deficit: 182.7 + 149.4 = 332.1. CRS attributes the $191.1B to P.L. 119-21, the enacted law; the Senate-passed text became the law, so there is no pre-enactment versus enacted-law distinction between the two figures. The earlier cross-check, which read 340 against 332.1 as a different vintage rather than a rounding of the same number, is withdrawn, as is the handoff's proposed edit 3h, which carried the same reading (D-13). ## Cluster 6 — Access and service-line context

Per the assembly instruction, this cluster retains only claims with a direct public source, and each retained claim is confirmed as stated in pre-existing and conditional terms rather than policy-causal ones. **AC-05 is now superseded**: the note's rewritten rural paragraph dropped the uncited cushion sentence rather than sourcing it, and the proposition survives only as a negative claim at NC-12. AC-01 is now sourced and its emergency limb removed; the three access citations the note carries are ledgered individually at AC-09 (GAO), AC-10 (Michelson et al.) and AC-11 (Lindenfeld et al.). **Updated September 27, 2026:** the migration reading at AC-12 and the rural consequence at AC-04 are superseded; the note's current wording is at AC-14 (shifting point estimates), AC-15 (the IMD exclusion) and AC-16 (rural).

| ID | Claim | Type | Primary source | Exact citation | Quote / table / cell | Measure definition | Scope | Horizon | Dollar basis | Scenario | Cross-check | Status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| AC-01 | Obstetric, pediatric and inpatient psychiatric services were shrinking or shifting before either rule was proposed | contextual [E] | GAO; Michelson et al.; Lindenfeld et al. | GAO-23-105515, October 2022; Michelson et al., December 2024; Lindenfeld et al., PLoS Medicine, July 2025 (data window ending 2023); note sec. 8 (opening paragraph) | Note sec. 8: 'Obstetric, pediatric and inpatient psychiatric services were shrinking or shifting before either rule was proposed (studies below)' | Pre-existing service-line fragility, evidenced by facility, unit and bed counts | service line | 2004–2023 across the three sources | n/a | not scenario-dependent | GAO is synthesising published research, not reporting an original count; the note's attribution was corrected from 'GAO found that' to 'GAO reported that research indicates'. The psychiatric limb does not support an aggregate contraction: total beds across both facility types were essentially flat and no bed type's average annual percent change was statistically significant (AC-07, AC-08, AC-11, AC-14; AC-12 superseded), and the note now states this. EMERGENCY LIMB REMOVED: the earlier version of this row claimed emergency services alongside the other three, cited by none of the sources; the note's rewritten sentence names obstetric, inpatient psychiatric and pediatric only, and this row now matches it. The three underlying citations are ledgered individually at AC-09, AC-10 and AC-11. Framing test: passes - stated as predating the rules and documented independently of them, with no causal claim UPDATED October 11, 2026: v17 words the claim as 'shrinking or shifting before either rule was proposed (studies below)' and no longer says 'structural fragility' or 'documented independently of them'. 'Shrinking or shifting' fits the psychiatric limb, where the authors found no statistically significant trend (AC-08). Still no causal claim. The three underlying citations remain at AC-09, AC-10 and AC-11 (psychiatric limb also AC-07). Previous wording: 'Structural fragility in obstetric, inpatient psychiatric and pediatric services predates both rules and is documented independently of them' | verified with caveat - cited at AC-09, AC-10 and AC-11; the psychiatric limb shows shifting point estimates with no significant trend, not aggregate decline (AC-14) |
| AC-02 | Where hospitals run obstetric, pediatric or psychiatric services below cost and cover the loss from other revenue, lower SDPs are a plausible pressure point | contextual (conditional) [C] | [FIRM] | Note sec. 8 (IMD paragraph, last sentence) | Note sec. 8 | A conditional mechanism, not a prediction | service line | prospective | n/a | conditional | Framing test: passes — the note states in the next sentence that it is not a prediction and that public data cannot convert it into one UPDATED October 11, 2026: v17 tags the conditional [C] (depends on facts not yet public: which hospitals run these services below cost). The earlier 'cross-subsidized within a system' and 'reduction in supplemental payment capacity' are replaced by 'cover the loss from other revenue' and 'lower SDPs'. Each row is scoped in full at the corresponding entry in Section C. No negative claim in the note is stated as a claim about the world; each is a claim about a search, or — at NC-12 — about a named and bounded set of cited sources.

# SECTION B — DECISION LOG

Each entry records a direction change, the output that triggered it, the rationale, and the result of checking it against the available outputs. Where the record supports more than the instruction stated, the entry is expanded and the expansion marked.

### B-1. The national exposure heat map was rejected as the primary deliverable

**Trigger:** KFF's issue brief of August 14, 2026, "At least 37 states have Medicaid state directed payments for hospital services that could be reduced by the 2025 reconciliation law limits."

**Rationale:** KFF had published an equivalent product, with better underlying data, three weeks before the intended publication date. A second map would have duplicated it without adding measurement.

**Verified:** Confirmed. The V1 output retrieves and quotes the KFF brief directly, including its Methods section. The brief is the source of the note's own state figures at SE-01 through SE-04.

**Expansion supported by the record:** The state work was not discarded. It was demoted from the primary deliverable to section 4 and re-purposed from an impact map into a structural grouping — which mechanisms reach which states — a question KFF's product does not answer. The rejection therefore changed the deliverable's claim, not just its format.

### B-2. RIA table values were taken from govinfo rather than the Federal Register HTML

**Trigger:** The Federal Register HTML rendering of CMS-2449-P embeds its RIA tables as bitmaps with no text layer.

**Rationale:** Values that cannot be selected cannot be transcribed reliably, and a transcription error in a load-bearing table would propagate into every derived figure.

**Verified:** Confirmed by the note's own Limitations section, which states that the tables are "published as bitonal images without a text layer" and that figures drawn from them were transcribed visually.

**Expansion supported by the record:** The mitigation is stronger than the instruction recorded. Every annual cell of the three load-bearing tables was independently checked against CMS-2452-P's Table 13, which restates them in machine-readable text — thirty cells, all matching. This is what makes CE-23 possible: the check that validated the transcription is the same check that exposed CMS's own printing error in Table 13.

### B-3. The state figure was resolved to $264.7 billion

**Trigger:** CMS-2449-P's narrative at 91 FR 30452 states $264.4 billion, which does not close against the stated $510.1 billion federal and $774.8 billion total.

**Rationale:** Only $264.7 billion satisfies the identity, and CMS-2452-P independently prints $264.7 billion.

**Verified:** Confirmed and recomputed. 510.1 + 264.7 = 774.8 exactly; 510.1 + 264.4 = 774.5, which CMS does not print anywhere.

**Expansion supported by the record:** The problem is broader than one number. CMS prints the state figure three ways across the two documents; CMS-2452-P's Table 13 prints −$246.7 billion as a ten-year total while its own cells sum to −$264.7 billion; $264.4 billion separately denotes the state impact of Alternative Scenario 2 seven pages later; and the same narrative-under-table pattern recurs at 0.3 to 0.4 in the high scenario ($339.6B against $340.0B) and Alternative 1 ($258.7B against $259.0B). The decision was therefore not a one-off correction but the adoption of a rule — prefer cell sums to narrative restatements throughout — and CE-27 notes that the resulting 0.4 gaps slightly exceed the note's own stated 0.3 noise floor.

### B-4. The framing shifted from auditing the $681 billion to reconciling CMS's two net provider figures

**Trigger:** The sector-wide survey of published claims.

**Rationale:** The survey established that careful gross-versus-net analysis already exists in several places, so a debunking piece would have added a seventh scattered instance rather than the missing synthesis.

**Verified:** Confirmed. Section 3c of the note (section 3b before the restructure) names the sources the survey found: CMS's own RIA, the Federation of American Hospitals' July 21 comment letter, the Texas Hospital Association's paired gross and net figures, HFMA, Georgetown CCF and KFF. *Updated September 27, 2026:* the note formerly said "at least seven sources"; it now says the work "sits across six sources" (NC-13), which matches the six named.

**Expansion supported by the record:** This is the decision that produced the note's title. Once the finding became "the discipline exists but has never been assembled," the deliverable became a reconciliation rather than a correction, and the $681 billion moved from the subject of the note to one example within section 3a (now section 3b).

### B-5. The $681 billion was established as POLITICO's arithmetic, not a hospital-association estimate

**Trigger:** The provenance search returned no independent derivation predating September 2, 2026.

**Rationale:** Attributing the figure to a hospital association would have been a factual error and would have implied an institutional model that does not exist.

**Verified:** Confirmed. The construction is stated in POLITICO's own article, and the arithmetic closes exactly: 756 + 265 − 340 = 681.

**Expansion supported by the record:** The search scope is recorded at C-4 and is what makes the negative claim defensible. The substantive finding that followed is stronger than the attribution finding: the construction takes both rules on the federal side and one rule on the state side, and omits CMS's own $154.9 billion overlap. ~~Consistent arithmetic would produce a larger number, which is why the note attributes the figure to quick assembly rather than to engineering.~~ *SUPERSEDED September 27, 2026 (D-14): consistent arithmetic produces a smaller number, not a larger one (SB-07), and the note no longer infers quick assembly. It says only that it attributes the figure "to construction, not intent" (SB-25), and that correcting the scope asymmetry would change the figure but not the basis mismatch (SB-22).*

### B-6. Table 12's provider row was established as a net figure, which identified the $56.6 billion as a double subtraction

**Trigger:** Transcription of CMS-2452-P's Tables 8, 11, 12 and 14, and the observation that each table's four entries sum to zero.

**Rationale:** ~~A transfer table cannot balance with one row net and another gross.~~ The states row is demonstrably net ($138.2B − $198.7B = −$60.5B), and the providers row closes the same way ($384.0B − $163.7B = $220.3B). *SUPERSEDED September 27, 2026 (D-14): the struck sentence is logically unsound, because a table can balance with a gross provider row. The note replaced it with CMS's caption for Table 12, "Projected Net Changes Related to Changes in Provider Tax Revenues and Expenditures" (CE-36, CE-37).*

**Verified:** Confirmed and recomputed; both identities close to the decimal. Corroborated independently by Georgetown CCF, which reports the $220.3 billion and states in the same sentence that it is "after taking into account the reduction in the taxes paid by providers."

**Expansion supported by the record — this is the project's largest reversal.** The earlier P4 output had reached materially different conclusions: that the $220.3 billion "originates from the Healthcare Financial Management Association's own coverage ... not from CMS's headline savings estimate," that its producer was "HFMA, summarizing/calculating from CMS's RIA," and that it "does not match any of the three specific CMS quantities you listed." Reading the RIA tables directly overturned all three. The figure is CMS's own Table 12 provider line; Georgetown attributes it to CMS rather than to HFMA; and it reconciles exactly with Table 14 through the $241.9 billion overlap. The lesson recorded by this reversal is that secondary-source provenance work established where a figure was circulating but could not establish what it measured, and only transcription of the primary tables could.

~~**A residual the reversal did not close:** HFMA's stated baseline — "no OBBBA and no proposed rule" — still does not match Table 12's baseline, in which the reconciliation law is already in place, even though the numeral matches to the decimal. The note records this at section 3 (now 3a) and leaves it unresolved. See TP-05.~~ *SUPERSEDED September 27, 2026 (D-14, D-16): there was no residual. CMS used a "pre-statute" baseline and captions Tables 5, 9 and 10 "Absent the Effects of the WFTC Legislation", so HFMA's baseline description matches CMS's. The note's section 3a now says so. See TP-23; TP-05 and D-9 are superseded and resolved respectively.*

### B-7. Publication moved to before the CMS-2452-P comment close rather than after it

**Trigger:** The regulatory status check.

**Rationale:** Comments post on the agency's own schedule, so the docket would not be obtainable in usable form either way. Waiting would have delayed publication without improving the evidence.

**Verified:** Confirmed. The P1 output establishes that the comment period runs to September 21, 2026 and that only 20 comments had posted as of September 5 — against 6,344 in the closed SDP docket — which supports the premise that the substantive filings had not yet arrived and would not arrive predictably.

**Expansion supported by the record:** The decision has a cost, and the note carries it explicitly rather than silently. It is stated in the Limitations section, in section 6's sixth gap, and again in Section C-11 of this ledger: every negative claim about docket CMS-2026-2476 is bounded by what had posted as of publication.

### B-8. A state grouping error understating exposure for four expansion states was corrected

**Trigger:** Review of the state classification against expansion status and the provider tax indicator.

**Rationale:** Colorado, Connecticut, Indiana and Vermont have no hospital SDPs but are expansion states with a hospital tax above 3.5%. Any grouping that placed them in a residual category risked reading as "no exposure" when one of the two mechanisms reaches them.

**Verified:** Confirmed against C4, which shows all four as expansion states with hospital_tax_above_3_5 = Yes and no hospital SDPs. The note now carries the correction as a bolded paragraph — "Group E is not a no-exposure group" — rather than a footnote.

**Expansion supported by the record:** The correction changed the classification's organizing principle, not just four rows. The grouping was reframed around which mechanisms reach which states, a structural question answerable from public data, rather than around expected impact, which section 6 establishes is not answerable at all. That reframing is what makes section 4 consistent with the note's title.

### B-9. The four-tier exposure scheme was abandoned in favor of mechanism groups *(added — supported by the record)*

**Trigger:** The threshold-sensitivity analysis in the C4 response.

**Rationale:** The tier scheme depended on a 70% cut point that its own author documented as unstable. Eleven of 46 assessable states — 24% — change tier somewhere across the 0.60 to 0.80 span, and moving the cut from 0.70 to 0.80 alone reclassifies nine states, because 0.80 slices through the densest part of the distribution: Nevada 0.8003, Illinois 0.8040, Arizona, California and Hawaii all 0.8042, Pennsylvania 0.8048 — six states inside half a percentage point. The response also flags that Arizona, California and Hawaii report identical shares to four decimals despite spending differing by a factor of thirty, as do Texas and Wisconsin, so ordering within the 0.70–0.81 band carries no real precision.

**Why this is a separate decision from B-1:** A classification that moves for a quarter of states on the choice of a round number cannot carry a published exposure claim, independent of whether KFF had already published a map. Either reason alone would have been sufficient; the record shows both.

**Result:** The published grouping uses KFF's own likelihood-of-impact classification rather than a [FIRM]-chosen threshold, which removes the analyst's cut point from the deliverable entirely.

### B-10. Florida's treatment changed from exclusion to inclusion-with-flag *(added — supported by the record)*

**Trigger:** The C4 response identified Florida as "the consequential one" — a measured share of 0.9373 that would place it high, against an INSUFFICIENT source flag from KFF's 2025 budget survey non-response.

**Rationale:** Under the tier scheme Florida was excluded from tiering entirely, and the response warned that "its grey fill should not read as low risk." Exclusion solved a data-quality problem by creating a presentation problem.

**Result:** In the published note Florida appears inside Group D with its data quality flag disclosed alongside the other seven states, rather than in a separate untiered category. The flag is carried at SE-13 rather than resolved.

---

# SECTION C — NEGATIVE CLAIMS AND SEARCH SCOPE

Negative claims are harder to support than positive ones. Each entry below records the claim as the note states it, how the search was conducted, what was and was not searched, and the scoped form that the evidence actually supports. Where the note's own wording is already scoped, that is noted; where it is not, the scoped form should be treated as the operative version.

### C-1. Facility-level SDP linkage

**Claim as stated (v9):** "We located no nationally standardized public dataset linking any SDP to a receiving facility. SDP preprints are approved at the arrangement level." *Updated September 27, 2026: the note now uses the scoped form below; the earlier wording was "No nationally standardized public dataset links any SDP to a receiving facility."*

**How searched:** CMS's published SDP materials, including the preprint guidance on Medicaid.gov; KFF's August 2026 SDP file; and the state sources cited in the note.

**Searched:** Federal SDP program materials and the KFF preprint-derived dataset, which is the most granular public compilation located.
**Not searched:** State Medicaid agency records requested directly; managed care organization contracts; proprietary datasets; state public records requests. Any of these might link arrangements to facilities in a specific state.

**Scoped form:** No standardized *national* public dataset mapping SDP arrangements to receiving facilities was located in CMS's published materials or in KFF's compilation. This does not establish that no state-level linkage is obtainable, and the note should not be read as claiming it.

### C-2. Per-facility provider tax payments

**Claim as stated:** "No nationally standardized public dataset of per-facility provider tax payments was located. The $61.8 billion hospital tax figure is a national class total."

**How searched:** Both Regulatory Impact Analyses, the reconciliation worksheet, and published CMS-64 and MACPAC materials.

**Searched:** The two RIAs in full, and the named federal and MACPAC sources.
**Not searched:** State tax authority filings; individual hospital financial statements and cost reports, which may disclose provider tax expense at the facility level; state budget documents.

**Scoped form:** Neither RIA nor the reviewed CMS-64 and MACPAC materials publish a facility-level distribution of provider tax payments. The consequence stated in the note is the operative point and is unaffected by scope: because the net provider figure is a payment reduction minus a tax reduction, and neither term is observable per facility in these sources, the net is not computable per facility from them.

**Note on the worksheet:** The worksheet was not available when this ledger was built, so its contribution to this negative claim could not be re-verified.

### C-3. Commercial rate dataset

**Claim as stated:** "No nationally standardized public commercial rate dataset was located." CMS's scenarios turn on assumed commercial-to-Medicare ratios of 1.37, 1.89 and 2.55 inpatient and 1.55, 2.63 and 3.58 outpatient.

**How searched:** CMS's materials for both rules and the KFF analyses cited in the note.

**Searched:** The two RIAs and the KFF analyses relied on elsewhere in the note.
**Not searched:** Hospital price transparency machine-readable files, which exist and are public but are not standardized across hospitals; Transparency in Coverage payer files; RAND's employer-plan hospital price studies; academic claims databases. Several of these are directly relevant and would be the obvious next place to look.

**Scoped form:** Neither RIA publishes the underlying rate data, and no *standardized* public source for checking CMS's assumed ratios was located within CMS's materials for the two rules or the KFF analyses cited. Given the named unsearched sources, this claim should be stated as scoped to what was reviewed and should not be read as a claim that no public commercial price data exist. ~~**This is the weakest of the five gap claims as currently worded and should be tightened before publication.**~~ *Updated September 27, 2026: done. The note's gap 3 now names the unsearched sources (hospital price transparency files and RAND's studies) and says neither was searched (NC-03). It adds that CMS varies commercial-to-Medicare and Medicaid-to-Medicare ratios together, so the range cannot be attributed to either set alone (CE-54).*

### C-4. Methodology supporting the $681 billion

**Claim as stated:** "A search of the AHA, FAH, America's Essential Hospitals, AAMC and Children's Hospital Association public records, the July 21 comment letters of eight named organizations in docket CMS-2026-1916, and post-August-15 publications from Manatt, Sellers Dorsey, Wakely, Health Management Associates and Milliman found no independent derivation predating September 2."

**How searched:** Named-entity search across five hospital association public records; eight named comment letters in the SDP docket; five named consultancies' publications after August 15, 2026.

**Searched:** As enumerated. This is the most explicitly scoped negative claim in the note, and it is the right model for the others.
**Not searched:** Member-only or subscription publications; state hospital association materials beyond those cited; ~~the CMS-2026-2476 docket, which was open and incompletely posted~~ *(superseded September 22, 2026: see the re-scope below; the closing-day batch not yet posted remains unsearched)*; any analysis published between September 2 and September 6 that had not surfaced.

**Scoped form:** As written. The note's wording already names the entities, the docket, the date boundary and the publication window, which is what makes it defensible. It supports "no independent derivation was located in these sources," not "none exists."

**Re-scoped September 22, 2026 (M-1).** As of September 22, 2026, none of the 28 comments posted to docket CMS-2026-2476 (IDs -0002 to -0029, posting batches through September 14, all attachments read), and none of the September 21 letters AHA, FAH and AAMC published on their own sites, contains a derivation of the $681 billion figure, a multi-state net-of-tax estimate, or a reconciliation of CMS's overlap or net provider figures. Closing-day letters not yet posted were not reviewed. *Updated September 27, 2026: the note now states that posted comments were reviewed only for the $681 billion figure. The review for other terms recorded here and at C-9 is ledger-only context the note does not rely on.*

*Method:* the regulations.gov full-text search does not index attachments, so each of the seven attachments was read individually.

**Chrome sweep of submitters' own sites, September 22, 2026.** Ten organizations were searched for a September 21 letter on CMS-2452-P. Letters were found and read for four: America's Essential Hospitals, MACPAC, Paragon Health Institute, and the Hospital and Healthsystem Association of Pennsylvania. Each was checked against eleven search terms covering use of the $681 billion, a multi-state net-of-tax estimate, and any reconciliation or dispute of CMS's overlap, the 65 percent assumption, Table 8 or the +$21.7 billion figure. All were absent from all four letters, and none of the four attributes any figure for Section 71115 to CBO. The single hit, "overlap" in Paragon's letter at p. 6, concerns the overlap between the MCO and health-insurer tax class definitions, not the rule-statute impact overlap. No letter was located on the public sites of the Children's Hospital Association, the National Association of Medicaid Directors, the Illinois Health and Hospital Association, the Texas Hospital Association, the California Hospital Association (whose CMS-2452-P summary page is member-restricted) or the Illinois Attorney General (whose only related release, July 21, 2026, concerns CMS-2449-P). Those six results are absence from a public site on the day after the close, not absence from the docket.

*Method caveat:* the sweep read each letter through fetched text rather than a byte-exact string search. Absence is high-confidence but is not a literal-match guarantee. No single absence is load-bearing, because the note states its scope rather than a universal.

*Nearest passage, logged for awareness and not cited in the note:* the Hospital and Healthsystem Association of Pennsylvania's letter (p. 1) contrasts CMS's projected federal savings of about $246 billion with an estimated $183 billion associated with the statutory changes. The $183 billion is rounded and unattributed, and the letter gives no derivation and no reconciliation with CMS's RIA. In [FIRM]'s reading it corresponds, rounded, to CBO's Section 71115 net deficit figure (SB-09) rather than its outlay figure (SB-14), so the contrast sets a spending figure against a deficit measure. It is not a reconciliation of the kind recorded as absent here and at C-9, and it does not bear on the $681 billion.

**Scoped form, as extended.** The note's section 3b (section 3a before the restructure) now states the extended scope: the 28 comments posted to the CMS-2452-P docket as of September 22, 2026, and the September 21 letters of AHA, FAH, AAMC, America's Essential Hospitals, MACPAC, Paragon Health Institute and the Hospital and Healthsystem Association of Pennsylvania. The six organizations for which no letter was located are not named in the note. Status: **re-scoped for the posted record; closing-day batch pending** (post-publication re-sweep from ID -0030, M-1).

### C-5. Litigation and congressional action

**Claim as stated (v9, sec. 7 item 11):** "As of September 22, 2026, no filed litigation challenging either proposed rule was located. Two bills introduced in July 2025 would repeal the underlying statutory sections, S. 2279 (Sections 71115 and 71116) and H.R. 4807 (Section 71115), and neither has advanced beyond committee referral. The formal opposition we located is in comment letters; press reports describe hospitals lobbying against the rules as well (POLITICO, September 2, 2026)." *Updated September 27, 2026: the September 22 wording ended "Opposition to the rules themselves consists of comment letters"; NC-05 is superseded by NC-15.*

*Superseded wording (September 5, 2026):* "searching the two rulemaking dockets and the trade coverage reviewed here located no filed litigation and no introduced legislation specific to either rulemaking; the opposition located consists of comment letters."

**How searched:** Both rulemaking dockets and the trade coverage reviewed for the note. The P1 output reached the same conclusion independently on September 5, from Federal Register, regulations.gov, reginfo.gov and CMS.gov. The September 22, 2026 currency check added congress.gov, which located S. 2279 and H.R. 4807 and confirmed their scope and committee-referral status.

**Searched:** The two dockets, reviewed trade press, and congress.gov.
**Not searched:** PACER or any federal court docket system directly; state attorney general announcements. A filed complaint would not necessarily appear in a rulemaking docket. Adjacent litigation located through other sources, Fifth Circuit No. 25-40766, concerns earlier CMS provisions and bulletins rather than either proposed rule, and is monitored at M-8.

**Scoped form:** As of September 22, 2026, no litigation challenging either proposed rule was located in the sources reviewed, and the two repeal bills located on congress.gov target the underlying statutory sections and remain at committee referral. The formal opposition located is in comment letters; the lobbying reported by POLITICO rests on one press report (TP-28). Because court dockets were not searched directly, the litigation limb is bounded by the reviewed sources rather than by the public record as a whole. **Perishable, and among the fastest-moving claims in the note.**

### C-6. OIRA review status

**Claim as stated:** "As of September 5, 2026, neither RIN appeared on OIRA's publicly available list of regulatory actions under review." (note sec. 1). Re-dated in the status box: "As of September 21, 2026, neither RIN (0938-AV69, CMS-2449-P; 0938-AV93, CMS-2452-P) appeared on OIRA's list of regulatory actions under review."

**How searched:** reginfo.gov's Executive Order submissions-under-review search, checked for both RINs on September 5, 2026, and again against the September 21, 2026 list in the September 22 currency check. RIN 0938-AV69 was confirmed on reginfo.gov as CMS-2449-P.

**Searched:** The public pending-review list.
**Not searched:** Nothing further is available; OIRA's list is the authoritative public source, and there is no public record of a transmission before it posts.

**Scoped form:** As written, and among the most defensible negative claims in the note, because absence from the published list is the observable fact rather than an inference from failed searching. It remains a point-in-time observation, now dated September 21, 2026, and could change without notice. A separate rule, CMS-2450 (RIN 0938-AV70), has been under review since May 12, 2026; it is disclosed in the status box and monitored at M-6.

### C-7. Hospital line within the provider aggregate

**Claim as stated:** "No published CMS table isolates a hospital line."

**How searched:** Both RIAs in full, as transcribed into the reconciliation worksheet.

**Searched:** Every quantitative table in the two RIAs.
**Not searched:** CMS technical appendices or supporting files not published in the Federal Register, if any exist; the OACT memoranda underlying the estimates.

**Scoped form:** No table in either published RIA disaggregates the provider category into a hospital line. This is load-bearing: it is the reason the $21.7 billion cannot be read as a hospital result, and the reason section 2.4's caution is necessary rather than decorative.

### C-8. The $56.6 billion in the primary record

**Claim as stated (v9, sec. 3a):** "We did not find it in either RIA's tables or narrative." *Updated September 27, 2026: the earlier wording also said that "the accompanying worksheet transcribes every quantitative estimate in both and contains no instance of it"; the note no longer mentions the worksheet here, though the worksheet finding still holds (TP-07, NC-08).*

**How searched:** Transcription of both RIAs into the reconciliation worksheet, then a search of the worksheet.

**Searched:** Both RIAs as transcribed.
**Not searched:** CMS fact sheets and press materials; any CMS supporting analysis outside the RIAs.

**Scoped form:** The figure does not appear in either RIA as transcribed. Supporting evidence from the C3 output extends the finding in one direction and bounds it in another: Georgetown CCF does not publish a $56.6 billion figure either, in its July 27 post or in the August 4, 2026 Senate Budget Committee testimony that references it — so the figure's circulation traces to HFMA and its downstream repetition rather than to a second independent source.

~~**Caveat specific to this ledger:** the worksheet was unavailable, so the claim about the worksheet's contents was not re-verified.~~ *Superseded September 9, 2026: the worksheet was searched in full and contains no instance of 56.6.* See TP-07 and NC-08.

### C-9. A consistent multi-state net-of-tax estimate

**Claim as stated:** "Paired gross and net figures already exist for at least one state; a consistent multi-state version does not."

**How searched:** The sector-wide survey of published claims.

**Searched:** The sources enumerated in section 3c (section 3b before the restructure), of which the Texas Hospital Association is the located single-state instance.
**Not searched:** All fifty state hospital associations individually; state budget offices; legislative fiscal notes.

**Scoped form:** No multi-state estimate on a consistent basis and horizon was located in the sources surveyed. Given that fifty state associations were not searched individually, this is a statement about the survey rather than about the field.

**Re-scoped September 22, 2026 (M-1).** As of September 22, 2026, none of the 28 comments posted to docket CMS-2026-2476 (IDs -0002 to -0029, posting batches through September 14, all attachments read), and none of the September 21 letters AHA, FAH and AAMC published on their own sites, contains a derivation of the $681 billion figure, a multi-state net-of-tax estimate, or a reconciliation of CMS's overlap or net provider figures. Closing-day letters not yet posted were not reviewed. The Chrome sweep of submitters' own sites on the same date added the September 21 letters of America's Essential Hospitals, MACPAC, Paragon Health Institute and the Hospital and Healthsystem Association of Pennsylvania, none of which contains a multi-state net-of-tax estimate; no letter was located for the Children's Hospital Association, NAMD, the Illinois Health and Hospital Association, the Texas Hospital Association, the California Hospital Association or the Illinois Attorney General. Paragon's request for a revised RIA concerns deadweight loss, not net-of-tax incidence. One posted commenter nets a tax assessment against directed-payment revenue for its own system only; that is not a multi-state estimate and does not touch this claim. Method and caveats as at C-4. Status: **re-scoped for the posted record; closing-day batch pending.** *Updated September 27, 2026: the note's item 9 makes no claim about the CMS-2452-P docket, and the note says posted comments were reviewed only for the $681 billion figure. The docket findings in this paragraph are ledger-only context.*

### C-10. A document reconciling the circulating figures

**Claim as stated (v9, sec. 3b):** "We located no public document reconciling these figures; that gap is what this note tries to fill." Section 3c adds that the discipline "sits across six sources, and we found no place where it has been assembled" (NC-13). *Updated September 27, 2026: the earlier wording, quoted below, has been replaced by the scoped form this entry recommended.* Earlier wording: "A journalist assembling three published figures under deadline has no public document to consult that reconciles them, because none exists."

**Scoped form:** No public document reconciling the five circulating quantities was located in the sources surveyed for this note. **As written, "because none exists" was an unscoped universal and should have been softened; the note has since done so** — it is the one negative claim in the note stated as a fact about the world rather than about a search. The scoped version is sufficient for the argument the sentence is making.

### C-12. The rural cushion proposition

**Claim as stated (v9, sec. 5):** "This note does not assess whether rural hospitals are financially less able to absorb these changes; the studies it cites track services, not margins or payer mix." *Updated September 27, 2026: reworded from "Whether rural facilities as a group have less capacity to absorb that disruption is often assumed, but it is not established by the three sources cited above, which document service-line capacity trends rather than facility finances or payer mix, and no other source is cited here for that comparison." The scope is unchanged.*

**How searched:** This is a claim about the note's own cited evidence base rather than about the literature, so the search is bounded by construction. The three cited sources were re-checked against what they measure: GAO-23-105515 reports the availability of obstetric services in rural hospitals and counties (AC-09); Michelson et al. report pediatric inpatient unit and bed counts (AC-10); Lindenfeld et al. report inpatient psychiatric beds per 100,000 by facility type (AC-07, AC-08, AC-11). None reports margins, operating cushion, payer mix or any rural-versus-urban financial comparison.

**Searched:** The three sources the note cites in section 5, as characterized in the note and in the available research outputs. The rural-designation mechanics at RM-16, RM-17 and RM-18 were also checked and bear on treatment paths, not on financial capacity.
**Not searched:** The rural hospital finance literature generally — MedPAC and MACPAC rural chapters, the North Carolina Rural Health Research Program's closure and margin work, Chartis and similar annual rural health reports, Medicare cost report analyses, and the Flex Monitoring Team's critical access hospital financial indicators. Several of these bear directly on the proposition and would be the obvious place to look. **The claim must therefore not be read as asserting that no evidence exists.**

**Scoped form:** As written, and the wording is already scoped on its face: it disclaims an assessment and states what the cited studies measure. This is the correct construction, and it is a stronger position than the sentence it replaced — AC-05 asserted the proposition with no citation at all, which is the defect D-4 identified. The note now declines to assert what it cannot cite, which is the discipline the rest of the note applies to figures.

### C-11. Blanket bound on the CMS-2452-P docket

**Flagged explicitly, and governing every negative claim above that touches docket CMS-2026-2476.**

The comment record for CMS-2452-P was open and incompletely posted as of the September 6, 2026 publication date. The P1 output records 20 comments posted as of September 5, against 6,344 in the closed SDP docket — and the substantive filings in a Medicaid financing docket typically arrive near the deadline, which was September 21.

Accordingly: every negative claim in the note about that docket is bounded by what had been posted as of publication, not by what will eventually be filed. *(Updated September 22, 2026: the bound now runs to the 28 comments posted as of September 22 and the closing-day letters located on submitters' own sites, as recorded at C-4 and C-9. The closing-day batch not yet posted remains outside it; see M-1.)* This applies with particular force to C-4 (no independent derivation of the $681 billion), C-5 (no litigation or congressional action) and C-9 (no consistent multi-state net estimate), any of which a comment letter filed between September 6 and September 21 could contradict. A state or association comment letter is exactly the kind of document that would contain a multi-state net-of-tax estimate.

# SECTION D — OPEN QUESTIONS, LIMITATIONS, AND GOING FORWARD

## D.1 Unresolved limitations

**D-1. Per-state hold harmless thresholds do not exist yet.** CMS announces final state- and class-specific thresholds on September 30, 2028. Interim data are due with the CMS-64 for the quarter ending December 31, 2026, and final data by June 30, 2028. Until then, no one outside a state can compute that state's binding threshold, which means the tax side of every state's exposure is structurally unknowable from public sources for another two years. *(RM-19)*

**D-2. RESOLVED. CMS's overlap figure is scenario-specific, and the note now says so.** *(Identified in the September 7 ledger; closed by the revision.)* The $241.9 billion overlap is cited throughout the note without a scenario qualifier, but it is the primary-scenario value only. The note's own paired ranges imply the overlap differs by scenario: the gap between the excluding and including series is 242.0 in the primary, 284.6 in the low and 180.4 in the high. Any use of $241.9 billion alongside a low- or high-scenario figure is therefore a basis mismatch. The note now carries that qualifier at section 2.3, and supplies the low and high overlaps as derived residuals of approximately $285 billion and approximately $180 billion. Those residuals are [FIRM]'s arithmetic, not CMS figures, and are ledgered as such at CE-33, with the eight worksheet rows they are differenced from recorded in the row. Recomputed for this ledger: the same differencing applied to the primary-scenario rows reproduces CMS's printed $241.9 billion exactly, together with its $154.9 billion federal and $87.0 billion state split, which is what validates the method. *(CE-19, CE-33, CE-34)*

**D-3. PARTLY RESOLVED. CMS's 65% tax-financing derivation does not close, and the note now reports that rather than reconstructing it.** *(Identified in the September 7 ledger.)* The note reports the parameter as "derived from 38 percent of SDPs reported as tax-financed plus a further 37 percent financed by taxes together with at least one other source." Those components sum to 75, not 65. Reaching 65 requires weighting the mixed-financing block at roughly 0.73, which is not stated. The note's response is to state the two observations and the adopted point estimate as CMS gives them and to add that CMS does not publish the weighting that converts one into the other — which is accurate and is corroborated by the worksheet, whose single row for the passage (R1012) carries the same three elements and no weighting. **The drafting defect is closed; the disclosure gap in CMS's own document is not, and it still matters, because the parameter drives the size of the $241.9 billion overlap.** The note also now quotes CMS's stated range of between 55 and 75 percent (CE-31) and records the three scenario parameter sets (CE-32), which is the fuller account of what CMS does disclose. *Updated September 27, 2026:* the note now reads the 75 percent upper end as 38 + 37 and says CMS does not explain the 55 percent lower end or the 65 percent point estimate (CE-49). It also uses CMS's word "SDPs" for the 38, 37, 65, 75 and 55 percent figures and "SDP spending" only for the range (CE-48). CE-20 and TP-19 are superseded. *(CE-20, CE-31, CE-32, CE-48, CE-49)*

**D-4. RESOLVED. Two access claims carried an Established tag with no citation; the note sourced one and dropped the other.** *(Identified in the September 7 ledger.)* The claim that structural fragility in obstetric, psychiatric, emergency and pediatric services predates both rules, and the claim that many rural facilities operate with limited financial cushion and high public payer exposure, are both marked as established but cite nothing, and no supporting source appears in the available research outputs. The first is now sourced to three named citations, individually ledgered at AC-09, AC-10 and AC-11, and its uncited emergency-services limb was removed. The second was not sourced but cut, and the proposition now appears only as a negative claim about the cited evidence base, ledgered at NC-12 and scoped at C-12. Cutting was the stronger choice: the rewritten sentence says less and can carry what it says. *(AC-01, AC-05, AC-09, AC-10, AC-11, NC-12)*

**D-5. Rate detail is imputed for roughly half of the hospital SDP spending analyzed.** KFF imputes payment rate detail for $39.6 billion of the $78.0 billion in federal hospital SDP spending in its hospital analysis — 50.8 percent — because 35 of 131 preprints did not include payment levels. Every state figure in section 4 inherits this imputation. *(SE-15)*

**D-6. CMS's own scenario range for the SDP rule spans $408.4 billion to $989.7 billion.** The high estimate is 2.4 times the low. ~~driven mainly by the assumed commercial-to-Medicare price ratios~~ *Updated September 27, 2026: CMS varies commercial-to-Medicare and Medicaid-to-Medicare ratios together, so the range cannot be attributed to either set alone (CE-54; note sec. 6 gap 3, sec. 7 item 1).* No public dataset located allows anyone to check the ratios. A range of that width is not a defect in the note; it is the object the note is describing. *(CE-04, CE-26, CE-54, C-3)*

**D-7. CMS expects to address the interaction between the two rules in future rulemaking.** CMS-2449-P defers the question and CMS-2452-P supplies it only partially. The $241.9 billion overlap is therefore CMS's current working figure rather than its final position. *(CE-14, monitoring trigger M-4)*

**D-8. CORRECTED — twelve states carry data quality flags, and two have no usable share estimate.** The September 7 ledger said ten. Re-derived from the tier file for this ledger, the count is twelve: five INSUFFICIENT (FL, KS, MN, MO, MS) and seven PARTIAL (AL, GA, SC, TN, TX, WI, WY). The earlier ten omitted Alabama and Wyoming, whose records carry a PARTIAL flag alongside a determined-zero SDP finding. Within Group D, three states are flagged INSUFFICIENT and five PARTIAL; Minnesota and Missouri have hospital SDPs but no estimable above-limit figure and are excluded from the $45.4 billion. Florida's measured share of 0.9373 sits behind an INSUFFICIENT flag arising from survey non-response, so its exposure is likely understated by its presentation. The note's corrected count of twelve is verified against the tier file at SE-19. *(SE-13, SE-14, SE-19)*

**D-9. RESOLVED IN HFMA'S FAVOUR (September 27, 2026).** ~~HFMA's baseline mismatch is unresolved. The $220.3 billion matches Table 12's provider line to the decimal, but HFMA describes it as measured against a no-OBBBA, no-rule counterfactual while Table 12 measures the rule's incremental effect against a baseline in which the reconciliation law is already in place. The note states both and resolves neither.~~ There was no mismatch. CMS states that it used a "pre-statute" baseline and captions Tables 5, 9 and 10 "Absent the Effects of the WFTC Legislation" (verified at D-16). HFMA's description matches CMS's, and the note's section 3a now says so. The earlier reading was overturned by the first adversarial review (D-14). *(TP-05 superseded; TP-23, CE-43)*

**D-10. PARTLY RESOLVED. The reconciliation worksheet has arrived; the state indicator file has not.** All 47 distinct R-numbers cited in the note and this ledger now resolve against the worksheet and match on rule, table, Federal Register page, value and series. TP-07, NC-08 and NC-11 have been re-checked directly against it and upgraded: the worksheet contains no instance of $56.6 billion in any cell of any sheet, and no CBO figure at all. Two residuals remain. First, two passages the note cites — CMS's stated 55-to-75 percent range and five of the six scenario parameters — are not in the worksheet at all, because it transcribes quantitative estimates rather than stated assumptions; both are cited to the Federal Register page directly (CE-31, CE-32). Second, the state indicator file (C2) is still unavailable, so state cross-checks continue to run against the tier file (C4). *(Coverage caveat, CE-31, CE-32, TP-07, NC-08, NC-11)*

**D-11. What this update changed.** *(New — this revision.)* Fifteen rows added: RM-21 (an uncited [E] generalization about docket timing, flagged unresolved), CE-35 (the thirty-cell transcription check, now verified against the worksheet), CE-30 (the five quantities are not additive), CE-31 (CMS's stated 55-to-75 percent range), CE-32 (the three scenario parameter sets), CE-33 (the derived low- and high-scenario overlaps), CE-34 (direction, not proportionality), TP-16 (the FAH comment letter), TP-17 (the Texas Hospital Association's paired figures), SE-19 (twelve flagged states), AC-09 (GAO), AC-10 (Michelson et al.), AC-11 (the short-term acute-care bed series), AC-12 (the migration reading) and NC-12 (the rural cushion negative claim, scoped at the new C-12). Eight rows updated: CE-02, CE-19, CE-20, AC-01, TP-07, NC-03, NC-08, NC-11. One row superseded and retained: AC-05.

**D-12. September 22, 2026 currency check.** No development since the note's date supersedes any finding. Status box added, with its first sentence amended as recorded at D-13; sec. 7 item 8 (now item 11) rewritten; three monitoring items added (4a–4c, now items 5–7); post 7 hardening applied (series R25); handoff edit 3g adopted in sec. 3a (now 3b) and extended with the Chrome sweep scope. Handoff edit 3d (tense fixes) not applied, because the status box handles tense; handoff edit 3f (Paragon footnote 5 addition; that footnote is now footnote 4) not applied. Handoff edit 3h not applied and replaced by the correction at D-13. Rows updated: RM-10, RM-21, RM-22, NC-04, NC-05, NC-06, NC-09; Section C entries C-4, C-5, C-6, C-9 and C-11; trigger M-1. Rows added, as context and not as note claims: TP-18, TP-19, TP-20. Triggers added: M-6, M-7, M-8. *(RM-10, RM-21, RM-22, NC-05, NC-06, C-4, C-9, M-1, M-6, M-7, M-8)*

**D-13. The CBO figures are one estimate on two measures, not two vintages.** The handoff's proposed edit 3h treated CBO's $191.1 billion and $182.7 billion as two vintages. Verified instead as one estimate on two measures, outlays and deficit, differing by an $8.4 billion revenue effect. The note's prior characterization of the $340 billion as a pre-enactment score and footnote 5's (now footnote 4's) pre-enactment axis were corrected accordingly. In the note: section 1's CBO comparison now leads with outlays ($191.1 billion and $149.4 billion, $340.5 billion combined, CRS R48633) and gives $182.7 billion as Section 71115's net deficit effect, replacing the former combined $332.1 billion, which set a deficit measure against CMS spending figures; section 3a (now 3b) describes the $340 billion as CBO's outlay estimate; footnote 5 (now footnote 4) runs to five axes; footnote 2 calls the $182.7 billion a net deficit estimate. In the series: post 4 describes the $340 billion as CBO's outlay estimate, with no values added. Rows updated: SB-08, SB-09, SB-10, CE-28, NC-11. Rows added: SB-14, SB-15, SB-16. *(SB-08, SB-09, SB-10, SB-14, SB-15, SB-16, CE-28, NC-11)*

**D-14. The first adversarial review.** *(Added September 27, 2026.)* Two independent reviewers read the note. Their findings, and the note's responses:

- **No combined figure.** The note stated no combined provider figure for the two rules, so the provider tax rule's +$21.7 billion increment read as the answer. The note now states the stacked figure: $916.9 billion in provider payments, about −$753 billion net of tax relief, bounded at about −$488 billion (CE-38 to CE-42). It also says in terms that the +$21.7 billion is an increment, not the combined effect (CE-51, CE-52).
- **HFMA's baseline.** The claim that Table 12 uses a baseline with the 2025 law in place, making HFMA's description a mismatch, was wrong. CMS used a pre-statute baseline (D-16). TP-05 is superseded by TP-23, and D-9 is resolved in HFMA's favour.
- **The transfer-table argument.** The third demonstration that Table 12's provider line is net, "a transfer table cannot balance with one row net and another gross", is logically unsound, because a table can balance with a gross provider row. CMS's own caption, "Projected Net Changes", replaces it. CE-13 is superseded by CE-36 and CE-37, and B-6's rationale is struck.
- **POLITICO.** Several claims about POLITICO's construction were speculative, or reversed under the note's own accounting. The note removed that the construction "suggests quick assembly" and that "consistent arithmetic would produce a larger number" (it produces a smaller one, SB-07). It narrowed the "not a hospital loss under any alignment" statement to SB-20. SB-07 and SB-13 are superseded, and B-5's expansion is struck.
- **Title.** The note's title was changed. The published v9 carries the placeholder "[TITLE]", and this ledger's title line reproduces it.

*Rows superseded in this update whose removal the supplied materials do not tie to a specific review pass are marked accordingly in their cross-check.*

**D-15. The re-review.** *(Added September 27, 2026.)* The corrected draft was reviewed a second time, and two further defects were found and corrected:

- **Attribution.** The combined figure was attributed to "the rules". Both RIAs score Sections 71115 and 71116 as the rules would implement them, against a baseline without the 2025 law, and neither separates the rules' own effect from the statute's. The note now attributes the combined figure accordingly (CE-38, CE-43). No ledger row had carried the "the rules" attribution.
- **"The article's direction survives."** This misread the article's thesis. The article compares two estimates of the same policy, as SB-21 records, and the phrase contradicted footnote 4's finding that the two sides are on incompatible bases. It was removed. No ledger row had recorded the phrase. SB-07's "runs against the direction" reasoning is the nearest ledger counterpart, and it is superseded.

**D-16. External verifications.** *(Added September 27, 2026.)* Three checks were made outside the supplied materials and are recorded as verified:

- **CMS-2450 at OIRA.** CMS-2450 (RIN 0938-AV70) was received at OIRA on 05/12/2026, per the reginfo.gov review record, rrid 1379863. This supports the status box's "since May 12, 2026" (RM-23, M-6).
- **ASPE's later version.** ASPE's September 2, 2026 version of its report carries the same $891 billion, $875 billion, $502–875 billion and 3.5 percent figures as the August 12 version the note cites (TP-10, TP-12).
- **The pre-statute baseline.** The provider tax rule's RIA (91 FR 46562) states that CMS used a "pre-statute" baseline, and Tables 5, 9 and 10 are captioned "Absent the Effects of the WFTC Legislation". Worksheet detail: Table 5's caption misprints WFTC as "WTFC" (I-05), and the worksheet transcribes the Table 9 and 10 captions ending "...OF THE WFTC" (CE-43, TP-23). The SDP rule's baseline was not part of this check; its limb of CE-43 rests on its table captions.

## D.2 Perishable and durable findings

**Everything tied to the rules' proposed status is perishable.** Explicitly:

- Both rules' status as proposed rather than final, and the absence of either RIN from OIRA's review list *(RM-10, NC-06)*
- Comment counts and comment period status in both dockets *(RM-04, RM-09)*
- The absence of litigation and congressional action *(NC-05)*
- Every dollar figure in both RIAs, all of which are projections in a proposed rule that CMS may revise
- The scenario ranges and the assumptions driving them, including the 65% tax-financing parameter, CMS's stated 55-to-75 percent range, the three scenario parameter sets and the commercial-to-Medicare price ratios *(CE-20, CE-26, CE-31, CE-32)*
- The $241.9 billion overlap and its $154.9 billion federal component, which CMS may restate in its promised interaction analysis, together with the derived low- and high-scenario overlaps that depend on the same tables *(CE-14, CE-15, CE-33)*
- The stacked combined figures, which inherit every CMS input: $916.9 billion, about −$753 billion net, the bound of about −$488 billion, and the low and high stacks of about −$259 billion and −$1,126 billion *(CE-38 to CE-42)*
- The state groupings, which rest on a KFF file with a stated as-of date and on rate detail imputed for half the analyzed spending *(SE-05 to SE-15)*
- All six negative claims, each bounded by a search conducted on a specific date *(Section C)*

**The accounting distinctions, the reconciliation, and the definitional separation of the five quantities are durable regardless of what CMS finalizes.** Explicitly:

- **The five circulating quantities measure five different things.** Rows 1 and 2 (government savings) sum to row 3 (provider payments removed); what cannot be mixed is standalone and overlap-adjusted versions *(CE-50)*. Federal outlays, state outlays, gross provider payments, tax revenue and net provider position are distinct accounting concepts. That is true of any version of these rules, and of any successor policy. *(Orientation exhibit, section 3)*
- ~~**A transfer table's rows are all net or all gross; they cannot be mixed.** This is what identifies Table 12's provider line as net, and it holds for any future table CMS publishes in the same form. *(CE-13)*~~ *SUPERSEDED September 27, 2026 (D-14): unsound; a table can balance with a gross provider row.* **A provider line is identified as net or gross by its construction and by CMS's own label, not by the table balancing.** For Table 12, the States-row identity, the 384.0 − 163.7 identity and CMS's caption "Projected Net Changes" do that work. *(CE-36, CE-37)*
- **Subtracting tax relief from a figure already net of it is an error.** If the $220.3 billion is net of the $163.7 billion, subtracting the $163.7 billion again counts the relief twice, whatever the final figures turn out to be. Whether HFMA's $56.6 billion was built that way is the note's reading, not a finding. The note allows that it may come from a source it did not locate. *(TP-24; TP-06 superseded)*
- **Two overlapping rules cannot have their savings added without an overlap adjustment.** CMS's own accounting supplies the adjustment; the arithmetic that omits it is wrong in structure, not just in magnitude. *(SB-05, SB-22)*
- **A federal-only nominal statutory score and a total-computable real-dollar regulatory estimate cannot be subtracted from one another.** The scope difference can be closed from CMS's printed federal shares, but footnote 4's five axes (baseline, dollar basis, fiscal framing, statute versus rule, enrollment and behavioral assumptions) cannot. They are a property of the two accounting systems. *(SB-21; SB-10 superseded)*
- **A provider aggregate is not a hospital figure.** No arithmetic converts one into the other without a distributional assumption that must be disclosed. *(CE-51, C-7)*
- **A range that crosses zero cannot support a facility-level loss estimate.** Whatever CMS finalizes, a point estimate drawn from a signed range without naming its scenario is unreadable. *(CE-18, section 2.4)*
- **The gap between federal savings and hospital financial impact is a data gap, not an argumentative one.** The three structural absences at C-1, C-2 and C-3 each independently block the step from state exposure to facility impact, and none is a function of these particular rules.

## D.3 Monitoring triggers

Each trigger is stated so it can be checked against the public record, with the work it would require.

**M-1. Comments posting in docket CMS-2026-2476. TRIGGERED September 21, 2026; partially resolved September 22, 2026.** *Observable at:* regulations.gov, docket CMS-2026-2476, from September 21, 2026. *Would require:* re-scoping every negative claim at C-4, C-5 and C-9 against the filed record; reviewing state and association letters for the multi-state net-of-tax estimate whose absence C-9 records; checking whether any commenter derives or disputes the $681 billion independently. *Resolution to date:* C-4 and C-9 re-scoped against the 28 posted comments (IDs -0002 to -0029) and the September 21 letters located on seven submitters' own sites; nothing found. C-5 re-checked separately, including congress.gov. *Outstanding:* the closing-day batch not yet on regulations.gov; a post-publication re-sweep from ID -0030 is scheduled for around October 6–9, 2026.

**M-2. Either RIN appearing on OIRA's review list.** *Observable at:* reginfo.gov, Executive Order submissions under review. *Would require:* immediate re-dating of RM-10 and NC-06; treating every perishable figure as provisionally superseded, since transmission to OMB marks the transition from proposal to imminent final action and a revised RIA typically follows within weeks.

**M-3. Either rule being finalized.** *Observable at:* the Federal Register. *Would require:* full re-transcription of the final RIA, since every CMS estimate in Section A is a proposed-rule projection; re-verification of the reconciliation, which depends on the relationship between the final rule's equivalents of Tables 8, 11, 12 and 14; and re-testing whether the net-reading identities at CE-36 still close. The durable findings at D.2 would survive; every figure attached to them would not.

**M-4. CMS's future rulemaking on the two-rule interaction.** *Observable at:* the Federal Register and the Unified Agenda. *Would require:* replacing the $241.9 billion overlap and its $154.9 billion federal component with CMS's revised figures; re-deriving the bridge at CE-17; and revisiting D-2, since a formal interaction analysis would establish whether the overlap is scenario-specific as the note's own ranges imply.

**M-5. CMS's announcement of final state and class thresholds on September 30, 2028.** *Observable at:* CMS.gov and the Federal Register. *Would require:* the first computation of state-specific tax-side exposure from public sources, which resolves D-1 and makes the tax half of section 4's grouping quantitative rather than structural. The interim CMS-64 data following the quarter ending December 31, 2026 is the earlier and more actionable of the two dates, and is the first point at which any of this becomes computable.

**M-6. Publication of CMS-2450 (RIN 0938-AV70).** *(Added September 22, 2026.)* *Observable at:* the Federal Register; reginfo.gov, for the RIN exiting EO 12866 review (under review since May 12, 2026, review ID 1379863). *Would require:* reading its state directed payment provisions and its rescission or revision of the 2024 managed care and access rules (CMS-2439-F, CMS-2442-F) against the note's findings; assessing whether it changes either RIA's baseline or the interaction between the two rules; updating the status box and note sec. 7 item 5 (formerly item 4a). Its agenda entry scopes it to program integrity and enrollment and retains state directed payments and payment and access requirements in its abstract; no official description of its SDP content exists, and no source ties it to the CMS-2449-P and CMS-2452-P interaction. Two EO 12866 meetings were held, both with home-care and HCBS groups. *Verified September 27, 2026 (D-16): received at OIRA 05/12/2026, reginfo.gov rrid 1379863.*

**M-7. SDP preprint non-renewal and approval actions.** *(Added September 22, 2026.)* *Observable at:* CMS preprint approvals on Medicaid.gov and state announcements. *Would require:* assessing whether financing effects are arriving through the approval channel before either rule is final (note sec. 7 item 6, formerly item 4b). Reference case: in September 2026 one state's directed payment programs lapsed at the start of its fiscal year over a dispute about provider tax calculation, and about $12 billion was authorized on September 18. The state is not named in the note's text or the series; the note now cites the Texas Tribune (September 17, 2026) and Fierce Healthcare (September 18, 2026) for the case.

**M-8. A decision in Fifth Circuit No. 25-40766.** *(Added September 22, 2026.)* *Observable at:* the Fifth Circuit docket. *Would require:* assessing the effect on hold harmless treatment of private redistribution arrangements, which the district court addressed in September 2025 by vacating 42 CFR §§438.6(c)(2)(ii)(G)–(H) and 430.3(e) and the 2023–2024 bulletins; updating note sec. 7 item 7 (formerly item 4c). Amicus briefs were filed June 2026; no argument date or ruling was located. A reported December 2025 Eleventh Circuit decision upholding the 2023 bulletin, which would create a circuit split, is **single-source and unverified**, and is not in the note.
